    **The OpenCode rows are a CLOSED enumeration, not a longer list — recorded 2026-08-31 (DELEGATED — operator's delegate, Decision 33, c3-s4-amend-1).** Three rows became six because three were missing, and a record that grows by discovery every time somebody looks is worth less than one that states why it cannot grow again. So the boundary pass's own gap method was turned on the block itself.

    **Corpus:** `bootstrap/install-opencode.sh` lines 560-655 at `2bc77ad`, the 96 lines `render_config` feeds to `python3` between the `<<'PY'` at `:559` and the `PY` at `:656`. That is the whole ownership surface, because the rendered file reaches its destination through a single `mv "$output" "$CONFIG_FILE"` at `:681`, the only passes between are readers — the host-contract asserts at `:657-677` and `python3 -m json.tool` at `:678` — and no other line in the file writes `opencode.json`.

    **Method, which is what makes the count exhaustive rather than merely careful.** The block emits exactly two artifacts: `config`, serialised at `:650`, and `preserved`, written at `:655`. A key can therefore acquire a policy only through a mutation that reaches `config` before `:650`, and in Python such a mutation must pass through `config` itself or through a name aliased from it — so the enumeration is closed by listing the ALIASES rather than by reading for intent. Five names in the block's scope are ever bound from the document: `plugins` (`:618`), `permission` (`:624`), `skills` (`:631`), `delegations` (`:636`), `servers` (`:644`), plus `owned_object`'s local `value`, which is what the latter four receive. `plugins` is never mutated. The statement inventory itself was taken by grepping the span for every assignment form and every mutating call — subscript assignment, `update`, `setdefault`, `+=`, `write`, `json.dump` — rather than by reading the block, so a site cannot be lost to attention.

    **Result: 29 statements, partitioned four ways.** SEVEN mutate the parsed document (`:600`, `:617`, `:620`, `:634`, `:640`, `:642`, `:647`). FIVE accumulate the `preserved` ledger (`:615`, `:625`, `:632`, `:637`, `:645`). THREE are sinks (`:650`, `:651`, `:655`). FOURTEEN bind a local or a constant and never touch the document (`:564`, `:566`, `:567`, `:568`, `:575`, `:597`, `:599`, `:608`, `:611`, `:618`, `:624`, `:631`, `:636`, `:644`).

    **Six of the seven document mutations are the six rows. The seventh is not a policy, and this is the sentence that closes the enumeration.** `:600` `container[key] = value`, inside `owned_object`, vivifies an owned CONTAINER — never a key an operator could hold — and each of its four call sites is followed by that container's own row writing into it: `:624` by `:642`, `:631` by `:634`, `:636` by `:640`, `:644` by `:647`. It can produce no key the six rows do not already produce, so it is SUBSUMED rather than omitted. **A seventh policy would have to be an eighth document mutation, and there is no eighth.**

    **What S4 must measure in the `win32 behaviour` column — stated, rather than left blank with no shape.** The merge itself carries no separator risk: `JSON.parse`/`JSON.stringify` are platform-invariant and this host has no `tomlQuote` analogue, so nothing escapes a backslash on the way in or out and, unlike the Codex region rows, the risk is entirely in VALUES — and only in values oso composes. Measured against that test, exactly one owned value composes a host path: `mcp.fallow.command`, which inherits the Codex row's already-measured divergence verbatim. Every other owned value across the six rows is a literal: `deny`, `allow`, the five `permission` verdicts, `context7`'s URL, `engram`'s argv, `$schema`'s URL, and `plugin`'s empty array.

    **What the enumeration also surfaced, recorded because S4 reproduces it and no row can carry it.** THREE refusals abort the render rather than writing: `:601-604` on an owned container present with a non-object type, `:612-613` on an `opencode.json` that is not a JSON object, and `:621-622` on a non-array `plugin`. They are behaviour rather than policy, and they are why `create-if-absent` reads "validate-if-present".

    **Residual, named rather than fixed, C3-S1.** `backupSizeKib` (`core/src/install/backup.ts`) sums `lstatSync(...).blocks` recursively, which counts a hardlinked file's disk blocks once per directory entry that links to it; `du -sk` counts them once per inode. Measured on a synthetic tree of hardlinks: port 588 KiB vs `du -sk` 196 KiB. Every existing backup is a per-item `cp -a` copy of individual config files — never a hardlink farm — so the divergence has no live path to an over- or under-budget retention decision today; left unfixed rather than special-cased for a shape the backup module never produces, and named here so a later hardlink-producing change does not rediscover it silently.

The switch pair is corrected to what the shell scripts actually read: `OSO_VERIFY_SKIP_SLOW` (`bootstrap/verify.sh` lines 135 and 171 at `97743eb`) and `OSO_VERIFY_SKIP_SMOKE` (`bootstrap/verify-codex.sh` line 979 at `2bc77ad`, `bootstrap/verify-opencode.sh` line 588 at `2bc77ad`) survive with their exact names and their `=1` semantics, read by `oso verify` from the environment; there is no flag alias for either, and none is invented.

The boundary is stated here explicitly so a later reader does not fold engram into the wrapper carve-out by analogy to how bash's own `install.sh` mixed both concerns in one file.

This is the SECOND time in this child that a pristine-worktree environment mismatch produced a published number CI contradicts, so it is recorded as a class and not as an incident. - **C3-D8. THE OPERATOR'S OWN DECISION, taken 2026-08-31 — C3-S6 splits into S6a and S6b, the three shared bash hook files leave this child for C4, and the bodies C3-D6 never named join it.** Taken DIRECTLY rather than through the delegate: the kimi-k3 delegate session this run names did not exist at the time — `ListAgents` showed only the idle Fable delegate five days cold — and the question turned out to be arithmetic against a frozen Exit line rather than a judgement of boundary. **Every number below was measured at `45625a7` on a clean tree BEFORE any slice was armed, and no file was edited to find any of it.**
  - **1. C3-D6's member list is too SMALL, not too large, and C3's own Exit line is what says so.** That Exit requires `bootstrap/` at **≤ 400 lines of shell/ps1**. Measured over every `.sh`, `.ps1`, `.awk` and `.bat` under `bootstrap/`: **7,172 lines across 25 files**. C3-D6 removes exactly one of them — `bootstrap/verify.sh`, 329 lines — leaving **6,843 against a ceiling of 400**. The bodies the list never named: `install-codex.sh` 1,039, `verify-codex.sh` 1,021, `install-opencode.sh` 860, `verify-opencode.sh` 654, `purge-opencode.sh` 617, `repair-engram-codex.sh` 485, `purge-codex.sh` 457, `repair-opencode.sh` 215, `restore-codex.sh` 124, `restore-opencode.sh` 124, and the ten files of `bootstrap/lib/` at 1,130 with `toml-regions.awk`'s 306 inside. Deleting those **7,055** lines lands `bootstrap/` at **117** — S5's four wrappers, `install.sh` and `install.ps1` at 40 each, `install.bat` 16, `verify.bat` 21 — under the ceiling with room. **The queue's own slice line always said this** (*"S6 CONTRACT delete bootstrap bodies, `bootstrap/lib/*` and `tests/hooks-test.sh`"*); C3-D6, inherited from C2 to close a C2 coupling, never carried it, and no slice had read the two against each other until now.
  - **2. The three shared bash hook files LEAVE this child, because they are not residue — they are live install payload and live shell libraries, in three classes C3-D6 never reached.** (i) **Trust and staging.** `bootstrap/hook-hashes.txt` publishes SHA-256 rows for twelve `plugin/hooks/*.sh` paths; `core/test/structure/published-hook-hashes.test.ts` derives that expected set from `core/src/routes/routes.ts`'s `GATE_ROWS` through `gateScriptsWiredFor`, hardcodes `plugin/hooks/lib.sh` and `plugin/hooks/lexer.sh` beside it, and its fourth case `readFileSync`s **every file it names**, so a deletion is `ENOENT` and a red rather than a stale digest. The bash side stages and verifies them (`bootstrap/install-codex.sh`'s published-path list, its `plugin/hooks/*.sh)` copy branch and its hash branch; `bootstrap/install-opencode.sh`'s `GATES_SOURCE`; `bootstrap/verify-codex.sh`'s installed-copy comparison) **and `bootstrap/install-codex.sh` sources `plugin/hooks/lib.sh` outright**, so the bash Codex installer breaks on the deletion rather than merely losing payload. The PORT carries the same surface and does not go with the bodies: `core/src/install/opencode-trust.ts` `PUBLISHED_TO_INSTALLED` and `openCodeGateScripts`, `core/src/install/opencode-install.ts` `publishedHashes`, `core/src/install/verify-codex.ts`'s published-hash read and its `["plugin/hooks/", "hooks"]` row. (ii) **Shell libraries.** `tests/plugin-lint.sh` sources `plugin/hooks/lexer.sh` as a library on its second executable line and is a `pr.yml` green-bar gate; `tests/opencode-behavior-bar.sh` sources `plugin/hooks/lib.sh` the same way. **`core/test/structure/bash-gates-are-dead.test.ts`'s own `RESIDUE_BY_FILE` already assigns both to C4 verbatim** — *"C2-S6 amended 2026-08-27: sources lexer.sh as a shell library; C4 retires the bar"* — so C3 taking them would be C3 doing C4's slice. (iii) **Citations.** Roughly eighty `plugin/hooks/*.sh` line-anchored `readFrom` citations live inside `citations-resolve`'s scanned corpus, concentrated in `core/test/port/lexer.test.ts`, `core/test/port/git-call.test.ts` and `core/test/port/gate-entry.test.ts`, and they are a THIRD conversion beside the two Decision 24d already rules.
  - **3. The security reading, measured rather than reasoned, and it is what makes the deferral safe.** **No manifest on any host routes a gate to a bash script.** `plugin/hooks/hooks.json` carries nine handler entries and every one of their `command` values is the literal `node`; `codex/hooks/hooks.json` carries nine and every one is `OSO_AGENT=1 node "__OSO_HOOKS_DIR__"/gate.js <name>`; the string `.sh` appears **zero** times in either file; and the committed OpenCode plugin spawns no `.sh` at all. The installed bash copies are therefore dead **at runtime**, exactly as C2-D18 recorded, and the coupling that blocks their deletion is install-time trust and lint-time sourcing — never gate coverage. **Deleting them is no security regression and no security improvement; it is scope**, so deferring them to C4 opens no hole and accepts no new residual.
  - **4. C3-D6's live-runner list is FIVE sites, not three, and the two it missed are nightly's Claude verify step on both legs.** The 2026-08-30 amendment names `.github/workflows/pr.yml`'s suite run and `.github/workflows/nightly.yml`'s bash-3.2 container run and Windows hook-regression run. Measured at `45625a7` there are also **two `HOME="$(mktemp -d)" bash bootstrap/verify.sh > "$report"` steps**, one on nightly's linux leg and one on its Windows leg, and `tools/verify-check-names.sh` consumes the report they write. `bootstrap/verify.sh` has one further consumer no list covered: `core/test/install/verify-claude-differential.test.ts` **spawns it** as the oracle the whole Claude verify port is held to. **The Windows hook-regression run cited `nightly.yml:142` sits at `:151`** — a line anchor that rotted exactly the way C4's own note above predicts, which is why every site in this decision is named by its command text and not by its number.

THE SPLIT.** **S6a — the suite half, atomic exactly as C3-D6 defines it.** Deletes `tests/hooks-test.sh`, `tools/render-hooks-json.sh`, `tools/hook-gates.txt` with `core/test/structure/routes-match-hook-gates.test.ts`, and `bootstrap/verify.sh` — which lands HERE and not in S6b because its hook-regression branch execs the suite, so the two cannot be separated. Amends `core/test/structure/bash-gates-are-dead.test.ts` rather than deleting it, since the gate scripts it proves dead are still standing after this slice. **S6b — the bodies.** Deletes the ten remaining `bootstrap/*.sh` bodies and all of `bootstrap/lib/`, re-points nightly's real-install and verify lanes at `node bootstrap/oso.js`, and retires the nine bash-oracle parity guards to bundle-only — which **§7 authorises by name**: *"after the CONTRACT slice the subject is the bundle alone and the fixtures stay as regression tests."* S6b is the slice that closes the `bootstrap/` ≤ 400 Exit; S6a is the slice that closes *"`hooks-test.sh` holds nothing"*. **C3 is TWELVE slices, not eleven.** **Scope refinement measured at S6a's own base, 2026-08-31:** `tools/hook-gates.txt` and `core/test/structure/routes-match-hook-gates.test.ts` move from S6a to S6b, because `bootstrap/verify-codex.sh` reads the table at six sites and `core/src/install/verify-codex.ts`'s `mandatedAgreementStatus` checks its hardcoded mandated tool list against that same table, and neither verifier dies until S6b — so deleting the table in S6a reds the surviving Codex verifier on both sides of the port. S6a still strikes the two mentions of the deleted renderer in that table's header.

The base both halves are measured against, read from the leg's own published numbers at `45625a7` (run 33422960499) and never from a rebuilt tree**, per the standing rule S5 left: ubuntu `npm test` **2,872** registered / 210 suites / 2,869 pass / 0 fail / 3 skipped; windows **2,774** / 210 / 2,579 / 0 / 195; the ubuntu OpenCode suite 118 of 118; the ubuntu hook suite `passed: 1641, failed: 0, skipped: 6`. **And the instrument's own shape, recorded because it has already cost this run three times:** the CI legs publish the node summary with `#` (`# tests 2872`), while a local TTY run prints the same numbers behind `ℹ`. The sigil is a property of the reporter's TTY detection and not of the suite, so a grep tuned to one place silently measures nothing in the other.

- **C3-D7. THE OPERATOR'S OWN DECISION, taken 2026-08-30 — the five shell-lexer divergences are CLOSED HERE as a deliberate, documented divergence from bash, in a slice of their own.** They are five input spellings that pass the production deploy rail, the commit-until-green rail and the push-off-run-branch rail while the shell executes the real command. All five are **faithful ports** — the frozen `plugin/hooks/lexer.sh` and `core/src/shell/lexer.ts` emit identical token streams for each, measured — so C2 could not close them without breaking C2-D2's record-for-record mandate, and C2 queued them instead. Three were found by C2's own security review and are worse than the two `C2-D17` pinned at the freeze, because they log no event at all.
  - **Why here and why now.** C3-D6 already deletes `plugin/hooks/lexer.sh` inside C3-S6's atomic contract, so **after that slice there is no bash left to be faithful to** — the question falls to C3 whatever anyone decides, and the only real choice was whether it is answered deliberately in this plan or discovered mid-execution by a child running unattended, where discovering it costs that child a set-aside. The precedent is C2's own: the heredoc class was ratified as an intentional improvement on the reasoning that the contract underneath C2-D2 is that the deny boundary match **what the shell actually executes**, and that reproducing bash there would be porting a bug and re-opening a live hole. The same argument carries to all five.
  - **A slice of its own, NOT folded into S6.** S6 is an atomic DELETION contract; mixing behaviour changes into it makes a red there unattributable between the deletion and the divergence fix, and S6 is already C3's largest and most coupled slice. A separate slice earns its own failing-check gate and its own verifier.
  - **The bar this slice carries**, inherited from C2's security lane rather than invented: each closure ships a differential against the real shell showing the port matches what the shell EXECUTES, and **the report states its corpus** — what was generated, how many, which construct classes were included and excluded. A clean result that does not report its corpus is not evidence. Both directions are proved: the spellings that were passing now deny, and the spellings that were already denying still do.
  - Splitting the class — closing the three new ones and leaving `C2-D17`'s two — was **rejected by the operator**: it is the instance-not-class failure this roadmap has now named fifteen times, and the two that would remain are the oldest rather than the least severe.
  - **C3-D7 amended 2026-08-30 (DELEGATED — operator's delegate, Fable 9eab15, C3-S5b's verifier round). The class is a `$` sigil before a QUOTING FORM, and bash(1) QUOTING lists two of those, not one.** Beside `$'…'` (ANSI-C) stands `$"…"` (locale-specific translation), and measured against the real shell under `set -x`, `vercel $"--prod"`, `vercel $'--prod'` and `vercel "--prod"` produce the identical argv. The locale spelling was reaching the production-deploy rail, the commit rail and the run-branch rail as a token beginning with `$` that no comparison matched — allowed, zero events, and it survived inside the `script`/`ssh`/`tmux` wrappers this slice closed. It is **pre-existing and identical at slice start**, so no slice introduced it; it is closed with the five because closing five members of a class and leaving the sixth is the instance-not-class failure the bullet above already refused. The rest of the class is measured and needs no closure: `$\`, `$\'`, `$\"` and a `$` before a space are the spellings where the shell KEEPS the sigil, and the lexer already reads them that way; `` $` `` is command substitution, whose word no gate can compute, and stays excluded by construction.
    **C3-D7 amended again 2026-08-30 (DELEGATED — operator's delegate, Fable 9eab15, C3-S5b's second verifier round). The residual this round replaces was WRONG and is struck: the locale form now DENIES UNREAD.** That text claimed the under-deny "needs the operator to have installed a catalog … a hole the operator opens deliberately". Measured, that is false. A catalog built with `msgfmt` from a two-line `.po`, then `TEXTDOMAINDIR=./loc TEXTDOMAIN=t LANGUAGE=en_US` in front of the command, makes bash run `vercel --prod` from the source text `vercel $"harmless"` — the real shell traces `+ : --prod` — while the shipped gate returned ALLOW-UNCOUNTED with zero events. Every step of that is a command an agent may run under the rails, so no operator need be involved, and a security assertion on an unmeasured contract is not one this plan makes twice. **The closure**: any `$"…"` span makes the command UNREADABLE and the gate denies unread, because the word bash builds there depends on `TEXTDOMAIN`, `TEXTDOMAINDIR`, `LANGUAGE` and a compiled `.mo` that no gate can see at judge time. That is the condition the lexer already denies unread for wherever an option hides the payload — a privilege wrapper with an option, an `xargs` null separator, a `script` invocation with no command flag, a session-creating form with a quoted payload — and the same mechanism carries it, with no second one invented. **The cost is declared, not discovered**: this REVERSES part of the previous round's over-count narrowing. `vercel $"deploy"`, `vercel $"--target" $"preview"`, `git $"push" origin main` and `git $"push" origin $"main"` now deny unread at the production boundary instead of passing uncounted, and `git $"status"` and `git $"log" --oneline` are counted as residue on the commit rail instead of passing uncounted; the tests that pinned those two shapes are rewritten to the measured verdicts rather than kept. The blast radius is bounded to the posture where fail-closed is intended: the production rail denies only while `auto=running` marks an unattended run, and the commit rail only counts.
    **C3-D7's class swept a second time, same round — every word carrying a command the shell runs LATER.** `trap` and `coproc` were live under-denies at slice start (measured: gate ALLOW-UNCOUNTED with zero events while bash ran the payload), and sweeping the class rather than the two instances found four more of the same shape: `mapfile -C` / `readarray -C` / `compgen -C`, whose callback bash evaluates while it reads; `alias name=…` followed by a use on a later line of the same call, which runs once `shopt -s expand_aliases` is on the same line; `fc`, which replays a history no gate can read and executes its `-e` editor argument; and `BASH_ENV=`, which names a file bash sources before a non-interactive `-c`. All six are closed with the two mechanisms already built — read the payload as a nested command, or mark it unread. **`complete -C` moves to the closed list, correcting this round's own record**: `core/src/shell/lexer.ts:28` carries it in `CALLBACK_FLAG_READERS` beside `mapfile`/`readarray`/`compgen`, and `:392-395` routes its option value through the same `deferOptionValueAsACommand` the other three use — the callback-flag closure already answered it, it was never a seventh member left open, and the record calling it "named rather than closed" was wrong, measured directly against the shipped bundle (`complete -C 'vercel --prod' mycmd` denies `prod-deploy-denied` on the current tree, allowed zero-events at slice start). One carries a command string and was measured NOT to run it non-interactively, so it stays named rather than closed: `bind -x` — the word gate's own output is the ground for that (below), not an assumption. `ENV=` was measured for the same shape and does NOT run for `sh -c`, `bash --posix -c` or `bash -c`, so it is left alone — closing it would have denied `ENV=prod npm start`, a shape a ported parity fixture pins.
    **The full reach, stated before any count.** 453 distinct command lines were driven through both gate bundles on both rails by a scratch-directory driver (loading `plugin/dist/gate.js` at `c4ccc2a` and at the current tree, spawned once per line per rail per bundle, state seeded per call — `auto=running\nsession=<id>` for the production rail, `mode=plan\nverify_green=false\nsession=<id>` for the commit rail — verdicts read from `permissionDecision` on stdout and the `events.jsonl` line each call appends). 399 of those are the committed lexer-differential fixture's own probes (`core/test/fixtures/shell/lexer-differential.json`, 399 entries, all distinct); 394 of them begin with the inert verb `:` outright and the other 5 open with a reserved word, a subshell group, or the negation `!` still wrapping `:` as the effective command word, so **none of the 399 can move a gate verdict by construction** — measured, not asserted: driving all 399 through both rails on both bundles (798 checks) changed 0 verdicts. That corpus proves lexer-argv fidelity against the real shell; it proves nothing about gate verdicts, because it never spells a production, push or commit verb at the position the gate reads. **The everyday list is what the reach actually is**, and it exists nowhere else in the tree, so it is recorded here in full — 54 lines built to cover each closure's mechanism:

    - *locale span making the command unread* (8): `vercel $"deploy"`, `vercel $"--target" $"preview"`, `git $"push" origin main`, `git $"push" origin $"main"`, `git $"status"`, `git $"log" --oneline`, `echo $"a b"`, `npm $"publish"`
    - *the ANSI-C decode reaching the rail's own denial* (7): `git $'push' origin main`, `git $'push' origin oso-run/child`, `git $'commit' -m x`, `git $'status'`, `git $'log' --oneline`, `vercel $'--prod'`, `vercel $'--target' $'production'`
    - *a wrapper option hiding the payload* (12): `script -qc 'vercel --prod' /dev/null`, `script -qc 'npm test' /dev/null`, `script -q /dev/null`, `script -a out.txt`, `ssh build-host 'vercel --prod'`, `ssh -p 22 host 'npm test'`, `ssh -i key host npm test`, `ssh -N -L 8080:localhost:80 host`, `tmux new-session -d 'vercel --prod'`, `tmux new-session -d 'npm run dev'`, `tmux -L sock new-session -d 'npm test'`, `tmux new-session -s pane npm test`
    - *a callback flag* (6): `complete -C 'vercel --prod' mycmd`, `complete -C vercel-deploy mycmd`, `mapfile -C 'vercel --prod' -c 1 arr`, `readarray -C 'git push' -c 1 arr`, `compgen -C 'vercel --prod' -- x`, `bind -x 'vercel --prod'`
    - *trap / coproc / fc / alias / BASH_ENV* (11): `trap 'vercel --prod' EXIT`, `trap 'git commit -m x' EXIT`, `coproc vercel --prod`, `coproc git push origin main`, `fc -l`, `fc -e vim -1`, `alias ll='ls -l'`, `alias deploy='vercel --prod'`, `BASH_ENV=./x.sh bash -c :`, `BASH_ENV=./x.sh bash -c 'vercel --prod'`, `ENV=prod npm start`
    - *the xargs separator* (4): `echo a | xargs -I{} echo {}`, `echo vercel --prod | xargs -I{} {}`, `echo a | xargs -0 vercel --prod`, `find . -print0 | xargs -0 -I{} vercel --prod {}`
    - *control, unaffected verbs* (6): `npm test`, `git status`, `git commit -m x`, `vercel --prod`, `git push origin main`, `git push origin oso-run/child`

    **Which shapes deny unread, which reach the rail's own denial, and which only count — replacing the false blanket claim that all fifteen are benign, all deny unread, and none is a deploy.** On the production rail, a denial's own message says which mechanism produced it: `"...past what the production boundary can read..."` marks a genuine **deny unread** (the boundary never resolved a command at all); any other deny message means the payload WAS resolved and **reaches the rail's own denial** on its merits — `prod-deploy-denied` for a resolved deploy, `run-branch-push-denied` for a resolved push off the run branch. Measured over the current tree's over-deny rows:

    - **deny unread** (24): `vercel $"deploy"`, `vercel $"--target" $"preview"`, `git $"push" origin main`, `git $"push" origin $"main"`, `git $"status"`, `git $"log" --oneline`, `echo $"a b"`, `npm $"publish"`, `script -qc 'npm test' /dev/null`, `script -q /dev/null`, `script -a out.txt`, `ssh -p 22 host 'npm test'`, `ssh -i key host npm test`, `ssh -N -L 8080:localhost:80 host`, `tmux new-session -d 'npm run dev'`, `tmux -L sock new-session -d 'npm test'`, `tmux new-session -s pane npm test`, `fc -l`, `fc -e vim -1`, `alias ll='ls -l'`, `alias deploy='vercel --prod'`, `BASH_ENV=./x.sh bash -c :`, `echo a | xargs -I{} echo {}`, `echo vercel --prod | xargs -I{} {}`
    - **reaches the rail's own denial** (18): `git $'push' origin main` → `run-branch-push-denied`, `vercel $'--prod'` → `prod-deploy-denied`, `vercel $'--target' $'production'` → `prod-deploy-denied`, `script -qc 'vercel --prod' /dev/null` → `prod-deploy-denied`, `ssh build-host 'vercel --prod'` → `prod-deploy-denied`, `tmux new-session -d 'vercel --prod'` → `prod-deploy-denied`, `complete -C 'vercel --prod' mycmd` → `prod-deploy-denied`, `mapfile -C 'vercel --prod' -c 1 arr` → `prod-deploy-denied`, `readarray -C 'git push' -c 1 arr` → `run-branch-push-denied`, `compgen -C 'vercel --prod' -- x` → `prod-deploy-denied`, `trap 'vercel --prod' EXIT` → `prod-deploy-denied`, `coproc vercel --prod` → `prod-deploy-denied`, `coproc git push origin main` → `run-branch-push-denied`, `BASH_ENV=./x.sh bash -c 'vercel --prod'` → `prod-deploy-denied`, `echo a | xargs -0 vercel --prod` → `prod-deploy-denied`, `find . -print0 | xargs -0 -I{} vercel --prod {}` → `prod-deploy-denied`, `vercel --prod` → `prod-deploy-denied`, `git push origin main` → `run-branch-push-denied`
    - **commit rail rows say "counted, never denied"** — the commit gate maps both `unread` and `residue` verdicts onto the same `residue-allowed` event; only a fully-decoded, gated git verb (`commit`, `commit-tree`, `update-ref`, `filter-branch`, `replace`, `fast-import`) reaches `commit-denied` on that rail. Deploy CLIs and unresolved pushes are never denied there — they are counted.

    ### over-deny, accepted, bounded to the armed run

    The option-only forms carry no command at all (`script -q /dev/null`, `script -a out.txt`, `ssh -N -L 8080:localhost:80 host`, `fc -l`, `fc -e vim -1`, `alias ll='ls -l'`, `BASH_ENV=./x.sh bash -c :`) and the remainder carry a readable command behind an option (`script -qc 'vercel --prod' /dev/null`, `ssh build-host 'vercel --prod'`, `tmux new-session -d 'vercel --prod'`, `complete -C 'vercel --prod' mycmd`, `mapfile -C 'vercel --prod' -c 1 arr`, `compgen -C 'vercel --prod' -- x`, `trap 'vercel --prod' EXIT`, `coproc vercel --prod`, `BASH_ENV=./x.sh bash -c 'vercel --prod'`, `echo a | xargs -0 vercel --prod`, `find . -print0 | xargs -0 -I{} vercel --prod {}`) — both shapes deny unread or count today, with no distinction between them.

    **Residual queued for the operator's presence phase, not the sweep** — it is a behaviour change on the security path, so it is not this round's to take: whether an option grammar distinguishing value-taking options from flags should let a readable command behind an option (the second class above) be READ and judged on its own merits rather than denied unread, narrowing `script -qc '…'`, `ssh host '…'`, `tmux new-session -d '…'`, `complete -C '…'`, `mapfile -C '…'`, `compgen -C '…'`, `trap '…'` and `coproc …` to their actual verdict instead of a blanket unread deny. If ever taken, it needs its own differential and both-directions proof — the shapes that were denying unread must still deny once genuinely unreadable, and the shapes now proven benign must allow.
    **The NEITHER state is closed by a gate, not by a promise.** The corpus asserted that no probe spells an excluded construct, but nothing forced a construct into corpus-or-exclusion, and that gap is what hid the sixth bypass and both of this round's blockers. The committed corpus now records bash's own vocabulary — `compgen -k` and `compgen -b`, 22 reserved words and 61 builtins — and two new tests fail when a construct sits in neither state: every one of the 83 words the shell reads specially must be answered by the lexer, by the list of words that run no command of their own, or by the list whose command only an interactive shell runs; and every construct the vocabulary calls exercised must be spelled by a probe while every excluded one must be spelled by none. Run against the lexer as it stood at slice start, the word gate names exactly `coproc, alias, compgen, complete, fc, mapfile, readarray, trap, {, }` — all ten, `complete` included, closed by this slice's sweep. **Run against the current tree** (`node --experimental-strip-types`, `shellWordsInNoStanding` from `core/test/support/shell-constructs.ts` against `SHELL_WORDS_THIS_LEXER_READS` and the fixture's 83-word vocabulary), **the word gate's own output is empty — `(none)`** — every one of the 83 is answered. `bind` was never among the ten the gate named at slice start: it carries its own standing from `SHELL_WORDS_WHOSE_COMMAND_ONLY_AN_INTERACTIVE_SHELL_RUNS` (`core/test/support/shell-constructs.ts:74-77`, `bind` and `history`), unchanged by this slice, so `bind -x` stays named on that ground and not because the sweep left it unfinished. Arithmetic expansion is now named in the exclusion list in its own right rather than caught incidentally by the command-substitution spelling, so the stated exclusions are what a reader auditing them will find.

**Proposed slices, SEVEN after C3-D7.** S1 EXPAND shared modules (backup/transaction, toml, json, trust, report) + `oso verify` for Claude; S2 EXPAND Claude install/repair/purge; S3 EXPAND Codex (TOML region parity fixtures from the awk cases); S4 EXPAND OpenCode; **S5b LEXER — close the five divergences per C3-D7, with the real shell as the oracle and its corpus stated**; S5 MIGRATE wrappers + nightly real installs; S6 CONTRACT delete bootstrap bodies, `bootstrap/lib/*` and `tests/hooks-test.sh`; the two bash bars (`tests/opencode-behavior-bar.sh`, `tests/opencode-contract-bar.sh`) are NOT touched here — C4 ports and then deletes them. Depends-on chain S1 → S2/S3/S4 (parallel) → S5 → S6, with **S5b depending on nothing and landing before S6**, since it touches `core/src/shell/` alone and S6's deletion must not be the slice that also changes behaviour.
  - **Slice list amended 2026-08-30 (DELEGATED — operator's delegate, Fable 9eab15, model Claude Fable 5, C3-S2b, Decision 26).** **S2b lands before S3**: engram release provisioning (asset table, injectable transport, `node:crypto` SHA-256 verification, extraction, placement) as `core/src/install/engram.ts`, shared by all three hosts since OpenCode's owned MCP set spawns `engram` by name too; `core/src/install/pins.ts` is created here (C4-D2, amended below) rather than at C4, holding `SUPPORTED_ENGRAM_VERSION`; Claude's `installClaude` becomes its first caller, replacing the "install by hand" wiring failure with real provisioning that only runs once detection fails. **S3's scope gains the per-host flag table (Decision 26c):** `parseArgv` becomes a per-host flag table carrying all four bash flags at once — `--yes`, `--replace-claude-md` (Claude-only), `--no-impeccable`, `--no-git-hook` — since Codex and OpenCode take the same three flags and S3 must extend the parser to reach them anyway, rather than growing it a second time once OpenCode's S4 needs the same thing.
  - **C3-S3 scope, measured and recorded 2026-08-30 (DELEGATED — operator's delegate, C3-S3 fix round 1).** The Codex port covers about a THIRD of the bash it replaces, not half, and the record carries BOTH denominators labelled so neither frame can be read as the other.
    **Whole-file denominator — the figure that answers "is the host installable", and the one that leads.** Ported bash lines over `wc -l` of the whole file; the ported counts come from a per-function pass (every top-level function span extracted, each classified fully-ported / weighted-partial / unported, lines summed).

    | bash file | `wc -l` | ported | ratio |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | `bootstrap/install-codex.sh` | 1,039 | 400 | 0.38 |
    | `bootstrap/verify-codex.sh` | 1,021 | 288 | 0.28 |
    | `bootstrap/repair-engram-codex.sh` | 485 | 94 | 0.19 |
    | `bootstrap/purge-codex.sh` | 457 | 162 | 0.35 |
    | **blended** | **3,002** | **944** | **0.31** |

    The four denominators, the 3,002 total and every ratio in the table were re-measured for this record (`wc -l` over the four files; the ratios recomputed from the pair beside them). The ported counts are the verifier's per-function pass. **Two figures an earlier report carried are corrected here:** it printed `322/1021` beside a ratio of `0.28` — `322/1021` is `0.32`, while `0.28` is `288/1021`, so the pair was inconsistent whichever numerator is right; and "roughly half of each bash file" overstates a blended **0.31**, which is closer to a third. **The blended 0.31 is published rather than declined** — it is the figure the scope decision needs.
    **Ported-surface denominator — fidelity, never coverage.** The same 944 ported lines over the spans of the FUNCTIONS the port set out to port, rather than over the whole file. Its value is near 1.00 by construction, so it answers nothing about installability, and it is named here only so the two frames are never confused: **the region differential — 570 cases over 57 committed `config.toml` shapes, byte for byte against the real awk — establishes fidelity far better than any line ratio can**, and it is the instrument to read for that question.
    **Measured fact, re-driven for this record, not inherited.** `oso install --host codex --yes --no-git-hook` over a fixture `HOME` exits **0** (`wired: 2, failed: 1`; the one failure is `fallow (mcp)`, which is not installed on the measuring machine). `oso verify --host codex` against that same `HOME` immediately after exits **1**, `passed: 3, failed: 5`. The five: `published runtime bytes` (`bad:19`), `runtime entrypoints executable` (`not-executable:2`), `Codex agents copied exactly` (`divergent:7`), `Engram Codex integration` (`incomplete`), `Impeccable Codex mount` (`missing`). Every one names a payload the port's install rail does not yet stage — the runtime tree, the seven agent `.toml` files, the two engram pointer documents, the impeccable mount — which is the 0.31 above restated as behaviour instead of as a ratio. `--no-git-hook` was passed so the drive would not rewrite this checkout's own `core.hooksPath`; no check in `verifyCodex` reads git config, so the verdict is the same either way.
  - **C3-S3 named divergence and closed corpus gap, recorded 2026-08-30 (DELEGATED — operator's delegate, C3-S3 fix round 1).**
    **Named, deliberately NOT fixed — `TRAILING_COMMENT`'s `.` against the carriage return** (`core/src/install/toml-regions.ts:4`). JavaScript's `.` does not match `\r`; awk's ERE `.` does. Measured through both callers: `compactHeader` strips the trailing comment and THEN removes every POSIX space, so a `\r` left behind at the END of a record is erased and the two legs agree — which is why no LF and no CRLF document reaches the gap at all (a CRLF record's `\r` is always its last byte; 45,000 CRLF comparisons found 0 divergences). It is reachable only from a record carrying a `#`, then a LONE `\r`, then more text on the same record: `[features] #\r[other]` compacts to `[features][other]` on the port and to `[features]` on the awk, and `  # \rhooks = true` is read by the port as naming an owned key where the awk reads no key at all. **Direction, measured on both instances: the port REFUSES (`FEATURES_EXIT`) where the awk proceeds** — all 76 recorded instances of this class go that refuse-safe way, so the reach is a stricter port, never a looser one. Left unfixed because no fix was provably safe against the full differential inside this round; and the shape is deliberately absent from the corpus, because that corpus is a byte-for-byte AGREEMENT net and a shape known to diverge cannot sit in it without either the fix or mislabelling a port defect as one of the oracle ambiguities in `SHAPES_EXCLUDED_BY_CONSTRUCTION`.
    **Corpus gap CLOSED.** The corpus carried no shape whose table header leaves the bracket depth unbalanced, so nothing measured what the port does with every line after one. `a table header whose extra opening bracket leaves the depth open for every line after it` (`[a[`, `b = 1`, `[c]`, then a managed region and an operator tail) is now the corpus's 57th shape by count, sitting at position 23 in `corpusDocuments()` rather than at its tail. The fixture regenerated from the real awk **purely additively — 560 to 570 cases, zero pre-existing recorded outputs changed, zero removed** — and the port answers all ten of its action invocations byte for byte the way `bootstrap/lib/toml-regions.awk` does.
  - **C3-S3b deferred half, parity claim and spawn class, recorded 2026-08-30 (DELEGATED — operator's delegate, C3-S3b fix round 1, Decision 31 ruling 1).**
    **The deferred half is NAMED WITH ITS INSTRUMENT AND HAS NO RESULT, which is what this entry says rather than citing one.** Decision 31 ruling 1 defers `bash-install × anything` to the only place the pinned Codex exists — nightly's real installs, activated in **C3-S5**, and **C6**. Neither has run for this slice, so **the deferred half has produced no measurement at all** and nothing below may be read as though it had. This sentence exists because R1's recurring defect was citing an instrument that never produced a result; naming the instrument and its emptiness is the discipline Decision 31 wrote the rule for.
    **Why the deferral is larger than it looks: `installCodex` stages NO PAYLOAD.** Measured over the whole rail (`core/src/install/codex.ts:199`), an install reaches exactly three DESTINATIONS — the managed `config.toml` region, the global `AGENTS.md` region, and the repository's `core.hooksPath` — and nothing else. It copies no runtime tree, no agent files and no marketplace payload. **Its own summary reports `wired: 4`, and both figures are right**, which the record states rather than leaving a reader to reconcile against the tool: re-driven for this entry with `installGitHook: true` over a fixture `HOME` and a fixture repository, the summary prints `wired: 4, failed: 0` over the rows `fallow (mcp)`, `managed config region`, `global AGENTS.md region` and `git commit hook`, while the only new paths under that `HOME` are `.codex/config.toml`, `.codex/AGENTS.md` and the transaction's own backup ledger (`.local/state/oso-code/codex-backups/<stamp>/format` and `/manifest`). The fourth row is `fallow (mcp)`, which writes an `[mcp_servers.fallow]` `command` entry INTO the `config.toml` the second row names rather than to a path of its own — so four wiring ROWS land in three DESTINATIONS, and the two words are counting different things rather than contradicting each other. **So four of the port verifier's checks assert staging the port's own installer never performs**: `staged marketplace payload` (`core/src/install/verify-codex.ts:116`), `published runtime bytes` (`core/src/install/verify-codex.ts:212`), `runtime entrypoints executable` (`core/src/install/verify-codex.ts:219`) and `seven Codex agents copied exactly` (`core/src/install/verify-codex.ts:391`). They are red against a port-installed HOME by construction, not by regression, and only the bash installer stages what they look for — which is precisely the half no instrument has yet measured. Nothing in the tree stated this before this entry.
    **The parity claim is CORRECTED to what the harness measures: kind-for-kind, never value-for-value.** `core/test/install/codex-verify-parity.test.ts` compares the two legs through `reportLines`, which reduces every report line to `{kind, subject}` — an `ok:` line drops its trailing parenthesised value (`core/test/support/verify-report-lines.ts` line 39 at `2bc77ad`) and the `FAIL:`/`skip:`/`note:`/`unverified:` kinds keep only the text before their em dash. So the harness catches an `ok`↔`FAIL` flip, a renamed check, a dropped row and a reordering — and it does **not** catch a wrong verdict VALUE. Demonstrated rather than argued: a report whose values are replaced with invented ones (`19 files, all forged`, `expected 9.9.9, got 1.2.3`) reduces to a byte-identical `{kind, subject}` list, so `deepEqual` sees no difference. **The claim reads kind-for-kind and must not be restated as agreement of verdicts.**
    **Both figures generated from the harness's own output in the pass that measured them, and they correct an earlier pair.** Driving the same fixture the suite drives: the harness compares **24** report lines; **20** of those carry a check subject; **19** carry a value on at least one leg; and the two legs **already disagree on 6 of those 20 values** in the pristine run — `Codex CLI version`, `Codex host contract`, `Codex permission-override contract`, `published runtime bytes`, `runtime entrypoints executable`, and the `fallow MCP tool drift` skip reason. **An earlier report carried "4 of 24" and both halves were wrong**: 24 is the compared-LINE count — it includes section headers and the summary rule and counts — and was never a denominator of values, whose denominator is 20; and the list of four omitted the two `unverified:` rows that embed the same version string the first disagreement carries, so they diverge for the same root cause and would go red alongside it. **This is why comparing values is NOT the fix a later reader should reach for**: a value comparison is red by construction on all six today, and the six split THREE ways rather than one — re-driven against the harness's own fixture for this entry, never transcribed. **Three** diverge because the bash leg quotes the shim's stderr where the port reports a normalised verdict: `Codex CLI version` (`got codex is a parity fixture shim, not an installed host` against `got not installed`) and the two `unverified:` contract rows, which embed that same string. **Two** are count-against-list rendering: `published runtime bytes` reports `bad:19` where the bash names all 19 files, and `runtime entrypoints executable` reports `not-executable:2` where the bash names three entries, one of them the literal unexpanded glob `.local/share/oso-code/runtime/hooks/*.sh`. **The sixth is not a rendering difference at all**: the `fallow MCP tool drift` skip reasons state different facts — the port skips because `no PR-gate check spawns <fallow-mcp> (G4)` (`core/src/install/verify-codex.ts:277`), never spawning it, while the bash skips because `tools/list did not answer within 10s` (`bootstrap/verify-codex.sh` line 547 at `2bc77ad`), which is the verdict of the bounded prober that DOES spawn the shim, waits out `MCP_DRIFT_BOUND_SECONDS` (`bootstrap/verify-codex.sh` line 339 at `2bc77ad`, default 10) and kills it (`bootstrap/verify-codex.sh` line 431 at `2bc77ad` and `:437`). **So the gloss below covers five of the six, not six**: the five above are one fact rendered two ways, and the drift row is two different facts — a later reader routing this must not fold it in with the other five.
    **The spawn class, re-counted site by site rather than pattern-matched, because a deferred class that grows while nobody counts becomes permanent.** Every `spawn`/`spawnSync` call site under `core/src`, enumerated with its first argument at slice start and now: **34 → 39, a widening of +5**. By target — `claude` 18 → 18, `git` **5 → 7**, `npm` 4 → 4, `codex` **0 → 3**, and one each of `curl`, `wget`, `npx`, `node`, `bash`, plus two sites whose target is a variable rather than a literal (`process.execPath` and the just-placed engram binary, both in `core/src/install/verify-claude.ts`) that a name-only count misses. The +5 is exactly the two new `git` sites — `gitRun` (`core/src/install/codex.ts:463`) and the verifier's `core.hooksPath` read (`core/src/install/verify-codex.ts:468`) — plus the three `codex` probes (`core/src/install/codex-host.ts:51`, `:57`, `:66`). **An earlier report's per-target figures are corrected here**: it read `git` as `8 → 10` where the enumeration finds `5 → 7`, and a total of `35 → 40` where the enumeration finds `34 → 39`; its `codex 0 → 3`, its `npm 4 → 4`, its `+5` and its two exclusions (`core/src/install/codex-host.ts:39` is a PATH lookup, not a spawn; the `git` and `npm` sites in `codex.ts` predate the slice and only moved) all hold.
    **An intermittent PR-gate red, root-caused and closed — `set -o pipefail` reading a writer's SIGPIPE as a failed lookup.** `bootstrap/verify-codex.sh` sets `pipefail` (`bootstrap/verify-codex.sh` line 3 at `2bc77ad`), and three of its membership tests had the shape `printf ... | grep -qxF ...`. `grep -q` exits the moment it matches, so the `printf` still holding data takes SIGPIPE and exits **141**, and `pipefail` promotes that 141 to the pipeline's status — where a bare `||` reads it as "not found". Measured, not inferred: `seq 1 200000 | grep -qxF 1` returns **141** under `pipefail` while the same pipeline with `grep -xF ... >/dev/null` returns **0**, because a grep that is not `-q` drains its input and the writer never sees the closed pipe. Under 40-way parallel load the live script reported a false `FAIL: the hardcoded mandated tool list agrees with tools/hook-gates.txt in both directions` in **6 of 280 runs**, naming a different engram tool almost every time — and it reds `npm test` through the new parity harness, which is the first thing in this repository's history to spawn this script from the test suite. All **three** sites of the class in this file (`bootstrap/verify-codex.sh` line 484 at `2bc77ad`, `:500`, `:514`) now drain their input; **0 of 240 runs fail under the identical load**. The two remaining verdicts the class could corrupt were a silently skipped mandated-tool check and a fabricated stale-row entry. **This is the standing rule above applied to a shell pipeline**: an instrument's own exit status was believed without being checked, and a `141` was read as an answer. **The class is routed to C4 by its MECHANISM, never by a command name, because the wrong name routes the wrong sites.** It is *an early-exiting reader at the read end of a pipeline under `pipefail`*: the reader stops before the writer is done, the writer takes SIGPIPE and exits 141, and `pipefail` promotes that 141 to the pipeline's status. **Six sites survive outside this slice's surface, all reported rather than touched — C4 owns those files**, and all three set `pipefail` at their own line 3. Five are `| head -1`: `bootstrap/verify-opencode.sh` line 12 at `2bc77ad` and `:293`, `tests/opencode-contract-bar.sh` line 11 at `8fb780e` and `:82`, `tests/opencode-behavior-bar.sh` line 12 at `8fb780e`. The sixth wears a different command and went uncounted until this round — `bootstrap/verify-opencode.sh` line 336 at `2bc77ad`, `head -n "$(wc -l < "$seed" | tr -d ' ')" "$global" | cmp -s - "$seed"`, where `cmp -s` exits at the first difference and SIGPIPEs the `head` still writing. **The warning that prevents the wrong route:** the four `grep -q` sites in those same files — `tests/opencode-contract-bar.sh` line 405 at `8fb780e` and `:431`, `tests/opencode-behavior-bar.sh` line 82 at `8fb780e` and `:320` — are NOT of this class. Each hands `grep` a FILE argument, so there is no pipeline, no writer and no SIGPIPE to promote; a reader who takes the class to be *`grep -q`* rather than *early-exiting pipe reader* routes all four wrongly. The same discrimination holds inside the file this round fixed: `bootstrap/verify-codex.sh` line 429 at `2bc77ad` (`grep -q '"id":2,"result"' "$out"`) and `:440` (`line="$(grep -m1 '"id":2,"result"' "$out")"`) read a file as simple commands and were correctly left alone, while the three PIPELINE sites at `:484`, `:500` and `:514` were the ones fixed. **C3-S6 deletes `bootstrap/verify-codex.sh`**; the fix is made because the file is live in CI until it does.
    **Two scopes for one key, kept different DELIBERATELY, which the tree did not say before.** The install rail captures, writes and restores `core.hooksPath` at `--local` scope, because a rollback may only undo what the install itself wrote — reading the full cascade and writing that value back would MATERIALISE an operator's global setting into the repository's local config, which is a change, not a restore. The verifier reads the same key WITHOUT `--local` (`core/src/install/verify-codex.ts:468`), because its question is the other one: which hooks path git will actually resolve, and a repository wired from global config is wired. Both are right for their own question; this round made the install rail's write explicitly `--local` so the write, the capture and the restore all name one scope rather than leaning on git's default.
    **The restore's acceptance of exit 5 is correct for a REASON the constant's name cannot carry, so the reason is recorded here.** `restoreGitHooksPath` (`core/src/install/codex.ts:445`) accepts `status === 0 || (!capture.present && status === 5)`, and `git config`'s documented statuses give 5 TWO readings — "you try to unset an option which does not exist" and "you try to unset/set an option for which multiple lines match" — so a name like `GIT_CONFIG_UNSET_MATCHED_NOTHING` asserts one of them without saying why the other is impossible. Two independent reasons, both measured on git 2.55.0 against a throwaway fixture repository, make the acceptance safe. First, the SCOPE: the 5 is accepted only when `!capture.present`, and `capture.present` is false exactly when the capturing `config --local --get` exited non-zero (measured: 1 on an absent key), so zero local lines existed at capture and the multiple-lines reading cannot apply. Second, the FLAG: the multiple-lines reading does not produce 5 for the flag this code actually uses — `config --local --unset-all` over two matching lines exits **0** (unsetting all of them is its whole job), while it is the singular `--unset` over two matching lines, and a plain `set` without `--replace-all` over two matching lines, that exit 5. `--unset-all` returns 5 here only for the absent-key reading. The restore's other branch writes with a plain `config --local core.hooksPath <value>` and accepts 0 alone, so the 5 that branch COULD raise is correctly reported as a failed restore.
    **The windows-latest red at `67c70ad` was a TEST over-specifying its own subject, and the product bytes it tripped over STAND.** Run `33356561894`, read by ordinal and location rather than off the summary: `not ok 12` at `core/test/install/codex-home-anchor.test.ts:77:3` — the leaf *the write grant names the state root this suite spells, never one nested under the Codex home*, failing at its FIRST assertion (`:81:12`) — and `not ok 65` at `core/test/install/codex-home-anchor.test.ts:49:1`, which is that leaf's parent describe and not a second defect. **The summary names neither and cannot**: it reads `# tests 2496`, `# suites 172`, `# pass 2435`, `# fail 1`, `# skipped 60`, and 2,435 + 1 + 60 = 2,496 exactly — so the `1` is the failing LEAF, while the parent's `not ok 65` lives in the 172 suites and is never added to it. **Two separator facts, not one, and the assertion could see through neither.** `codexPathsFor` joins natively (`core/src/install/codex.ts:88-95`) where `renderCodexManagedConfig` joins posix (`core/src/install/codex-config.ts:52`), and `tomlQuote` (`core/src/install/codex-config.ts:43`) then escapes every `\` as `\\` on the way into `config.toml` — so the grant row the assertion read carried `"C:\\Users\\...\\home-2/.local/state/oso-code" = true` where its `path.posix.join` expectation spelled a single-backslash `C:\Users\...\home-2/.local/state/oso-code`. Linux hid both, because there the two joins agree and there is no backslash to escape. **The repair is in the test and nowhere else, and it is the CLASS, not the two entries**: every comparison of rendered region bytes against a joined expectation now collapses each run of `\` or `/` to one `/` on both sides (`core/test/install/codex-home-anchor.test.ts` line 206 at `2bc77ad`) and then proves the ANCHOR — the state root hangs off `$HOME`, never under `codexHome` — which is the question the case is named for and one separators are incidental to. **The case's third assertion was not merely latent, it was INERT on win32, which is measured here rather than argued.** Under the old comparison a region rendered with the Codex home as its anchor — the exact defect that assertion exists to forbid — spells its grant `"C:\\Users\\operator\\home\\.codex/.local/state/oso-code" = true`, which a posix-joined needle cannot match, so the negative passed against the very thing it forbids; normalised, it goes red there on both legs. **Shown RED before it was fixed, on Linux, from a win32-shaped `HOME`.** The case at `core/test/install/codex-home-anchor.test.ts:102` drives the real renderer over `C:\Users\operator\home`, reproduced the CI failure on this machine in the same shape (same first-assertion column, same mixed `OSO_STATE_BIN` row), and is KEPT as the standing guard, so this class cannot go red on windows-latest again without going red on ubuntu first; the `provedSomething` beside it fails the day that fixture stops telling a separator-blind comparison from a byte-for-byte one. **The mixed-separator output is NOT changed and nothing measured asks for it to be** — it is the divergence the Codex ownership row above already names and accepts, win32 resolves either separator, and normalising it is a behaviour change owing its own differential rather than a repair owed by this red.

  - **C3-S4 armament rulings, recorded 2026-08-31 before the OpenCode port opens (DELEGATED — operator's delegate, Decision 33, c3-s4-amend-1).** Ruled ahead of C3-S4 so the porting slice is written against a closed spec rather than amending one mid-flight. Rulings (1) and (2) are recorded here, never implemented here — the tables and the fix are C3-S4's, C3-S4b's and C3-S5's work.

    **(1) The flag table becomes HOST × VERB — ruled once as a general shape, never per host.** `FLAGS_PER_HOST` (`core/src/install/cli.ts:17`) becomes `FLAGS_PER_HOST_AND_VERB`, populated from each bash script's own `case` block rather than by analogy between hosts. Measured for this entry, the case labels are: `install.sh` `--yes --no-impeccable --no-git-hook --replace-claude-md`; `install-codex.sh` and `install-opencode.sh` the same minus `--replace-claude-md`; `repair-opencode.sh` `--yes --list`; `restore-codex.sh` and `restore-opencode.sh` `--yes --list`; `purge-codex.sh` `--yes --restore`; `purge-opencode.sh` `--yes --dry-run --keep-gentle-ai --restore`. Today's table carries the INSTALL row for every verb of a host alike, so it can express none of `--list`, `--dry-run`, `--keep-gentle-ai`, `--restore` or a positional.

    **NOTHING is dropped.** `--dry-run`, `--keep-gentle-ai`, `--restore <dir>`, `--list` and the positional backup name all carry — dropping an operator capability fails the first priority, and `--keep-gentle-ai` is host-unique and so cannot be narrowed by analogy to a Codex flag that does not exist. **A deliberately dropped flag, if one ever exists, is a recorded divergence naming the flag and the reason — never a silent absence.**

    **Positional arguments are declared per VERB, and the table keeps two shapes distinct.** The measured positional is `bootstrap/restore-opencode.sh` lines 38-41 at `2bc77ad` and its `bootstrap/restore-codex.sh` twin: a bare `*)` arm taking one backup name, guarded by `only one backup name may be given`. `--restore <dir>` in the two purges is an option-ARGUMENT rather than a positional (`RESTORE_BACKUP=$2`, then `shift 2`), and collapsing the two shapes into one would silently change which token an operator may omit.

    **Pairwise exclusions are carried as DATA, with one refusal test each.** The measured example is `purge-opencode.sh`'s argument `case` block, whose guards are quoted rather than cited by line: `--yes cannot be combined with --restore`, `--yes cannot be combined with --dry-run`, `--dry-run cannot be combined with --restore`, `--keep-gentle-ai cannot be combined with --restore`, plus `--restore may be specified only once` (a repeat guard, not a pairwise one) and `--restore requires a backup directory` (an arity guard). Four distinct PAIRS, spelled by seven guard lines.

    **One measured ASYMMETRY, and Decision 34 DISPOSITIONED it rather than leaving it named: the flag table carries exclusions as ORDERED PAIRS, as measured.** Three of the four pairs are guarded from both arms. `{--keep-gentle-ai, --restore}` is guarded from one: `--keep-gentle-ai` refuses when the mode is already `restore`, but the `--restore` arm tests the mode, `ASSUME_YES` and `DRY_RUN` and never `KEEP_GENTLE_AI` — so `--restore DIR --keep-gentle-ai` is refused while `--keep-gentle-ai --restore DIR` is ACCEPTED. **The ordered pair is carried because a symmetric table would silently change behaviour, and this is a PORT.** The asymmetry is recorded as a documented bash quirk, and it reads like an oversight rather than a design — the `--restore` arm simply never tests `KEEP_GENTLE_AI` the way it tests the other two. **Queued as a divergence question for the operator's presence phase:** if the operator ever wants it symmetric, that is a behaviour change owing its own test, after the port and never inside it.

    **(2) `purge --host codex` narrowed `--restore` away silently — recorded as a DEFECT, not a divergence, and routed to C3-S5.** Re-measured for this entry: `bootstrap/purge-codex.sh`'s `case` block takes exactly `--yes` and `--restore <dir>` and refuses `unknown argument: $1`; `purgeCodex` (`core/src/install/codex.ts:296`) reads `input.assumeYes` alone (`:297`) and implements no restore; and `FLAGS_PER_HOST` offers `--restore` to no host, so `oso purge --host codex --restore <dir>` is refused by `FlagNotOfferedError` rather than ignored. The narrowing is therefore **confirmed, silent — no record named it before this one — and self-documenting**: the port's own exit line (`core/src/install/codex.ts:330`) reads `restore with: oso install --host codex --yes, or by hand from ${tx.backupRoot}` where the bash it replaces offers the one-command `restore with: HOME="$HOME" CODEX_HOME="$CODEX_HOME" bash "$0" --restore "$BACKUP_DIR"` (`bootstrap/purge-codex.sh` line 361 at `2bc77ad`). Re-installing is not restoring — it re-renders oso's managed region rather than returning the operator's prior bytes — so the line offers a SUBSTITUTE for a capability rather than the capability, and sends the operator to the backup root by hand for the rest.

    **Why a defect and not a divergence:** a divergence is a difference the record accepts with its reason, and there was no reason here — the flag was not carried and nothing said so. **Routed to C3-S5 as a named, BOUNDED item**, on three grounds that make it cheap there and dear anywhere else: it reuses the shared restore machinery C3-S4b builds, C3-S5 proves flag-completeness on nightly regardless (Decision 26c), and the exit line is corrected in the same pass. **Decision 30's warning stands, carried here rather than assumed:** if C3-S5's boundary pass finds this item pushes C3-S5 past its stated budget, **it returns as its own small slice rather than growing C3-S5 silently.**

    **(3) A half ships WORKING VERBS, not stubs of all of them.** `oso repair --host opencode` lands COMPLETE in C3-S4; `install` and `purge` throw until C3-S4b. `VerbNotImplementedError` (`core/src/install/cli.ts:45`) today reads `${verb} --host ${host} is not yet implemented in this slice`, which names no slice — it gains the slice that BRINGS the verb, so an operator who meets it mid-child gets a pointer instead of a bare refusal. Each half's exit lists its working verbs and its throwing ones. **The parity bar those verbs are proven against is (4)'s, closed by Decision 34** — and `repair` is one of the three OpenCode verbs measured to carry no binary gate at all, so it takes Decision 30's original condition 2 in full.

    **(4) The bar C3-S4 inherits is NOT C3-S3b's — CLOSED by Decision 34 (DELEGATED — operator's delegate, Fable 9eab15, model Claude Fable 5, 2026-08-31): Decision 31's Codex waiver covers NOTHING on OpenCode, and `install` is driven THROUGH THE SHIM as a first-class parity instrument.** Decision 33's bar claim reached that same verdict from two proofs that do not hold. Both were re-measured by the applier rather than transcribed, and both are **CORRECTED BY Decision 34** in the two paragraphs below; the verdict stands on the boundary that follows them instead, never on the proofs. **That the applier re-measured rather than transcribed is the mechanism working, and it is recorded here as such** — the lesson underneath was already on the books word for word: *a remedy is verified at the CONSUMER of the output it changes, not the producer's counts.* Decision 33 and the round that carried it both applied it one level short, which is precisely the failure that rule names.

    **Claim one — that `bootstrap/install-opencode.sh` line 90 at `2bc77ad`'s `command -v opencode >/dev/null 2>&1 || return 0` means the OpenCode bash install runs binary-less — misreads the level the absence is caught at.** The `|| return 0` makes `probe_opencode_version` SUCCEED WITH EMPTY OUTPUT, which is what lets it be called inside a `$(...)` under `set -e`; the absence is then caught one level up. `check_baseline` (`:97`) reads that empty string at `:100` and returns 1, and `main` turns that into a hard stop at `:809-811`: `fail "host baseline not met — upgrade opencode to $SUPPORTED_OPENCODE_VERSION and re-run"`. **The OpenCode bash install does not run binary-less; it refuses to run at all** — a version gate of exactly the shape Decision 31's waiver names.

    **Claim two — that nightly's OpenCode verify leg is a standing binary-less proof at `passed=16, failed=0` — measures something else.** What makes that leg's fixture self-contained is that it MANUFACTURES A BINARY: `setup_local_fixture` writes a `#!/bin/sh` shim at `$shims/opencode` answering `--version` with `$SUPPORTED_OPENCODE_VERSION` and `exit 64` for everything else (`bootstrap/verify-opencode.sh` lines 100-106 at `2bc77ad`), then drives `bash "$SCRIPT_DIR/install-opencode.sh" --yes --no-impeccable --no-git-hook` with that shim first on `PATH` (`:111-113`). The shim exists BECAUSE the install refuses without one, so the figure is evidence FOR claim one's refutation rather than against it — and it is the same construct the Codex record already names, `a parity fixture shim, not an installed host`. The leg also sets `OSO_VERIFY_SKIP_SMOKE: 1`, so the wave smoke contributes nothing to the 16.

    **What IS measured, and what it moves.** The binary gate is per VERB, and only `install` has one. `bootstrap/verify-opencode.sh` genuinely runs binary-less — `opencode_version_status` (`:61-67`) reports `opencode-not-on-path` as a CHECK RESULT and never aborts — and `repair-opencode.sh`, `purge-opencode.sh` and `restore-opencode.sh` carry no binary gate of any kind: zero hits for `command -v opencode`, `check_baseline` or `SUPPORTED_OPENCODE_VERSION` across all three. So the conditional the ruling was built on still stands — **the waiver exists where a version gate makes the bash unrunnable, and only there** — but it now resolves the OTHER WAY for `install`, the verb the ruling most needed it for.

    **The durable part, and the reason the verdict needs neither broken proof — recorded as the general rule it is.** Parity (port ≡ bash) needs only that **both implementations see identical answers**, so a shim is a sound parity instrument **when the gate consumes what the shim can answer**: **a shim closes a gate that asks a QUESTION; it does not close a gate that demands a BEHAVIOUR.** OpenCode's gate consumes a version STRING — one question, one answer, shim-closable. Codex's gate consumes sandbox EXECUTION (`codex sandbox -P oso -- /bin/true`) plus structured plugin and marketplace responses — behaviour no shim answers without becoming the 250–400-line unmeasured fake Decision 31 rejected on cost. The Codex record's `a parity fixture shim, not an installed host` flag is about FIDELITY, which stays nightly's and C6's claim on both hosts and is unchanged here; it was never a parity objection.

    **The fact that makes OpenCode's gate a question, supplied by Decision 34 and RE-MEASURED here rather than taken.** The bash install spawns `opencode` for **exactly one question**: `opencode --version` at `bootstrap/install-opencode.sh` line 91 at `2bc77ad`. `:90`'s `command -v opencode` is a PATH lookup rather than a spawn; `:101`, `:105`, `:108` and `:810` are message text; and `:699`'s `engram setup opencode` spawns a DIFFERENT binary, `engram`, behind its own `command -v engram` at `:687`. None of the four libraries the installer sources — `install-backup.sh`, `opencode-install-backups.sh`, `opencode-trust-bytes.sh`, `codex-managed-config.sh` — spawns `opencode` at all. **One spawn, one question, and that is the whole of what the gate consumes.**

    **Four bounds ride the ruling, and C3-S4 owes all four.**

    **(i) The shim's contract is STATED IN THE HARNESS, never assumed from the bash.** It answers `--version` with the pinned string and `exit 64` for everything else; the port's injected probe returns **byte-identically** what the shim returns; and **the harness LISTS the shim-answered calls — today exactly one.** The listing is the point rather than a decoration: the parity claim then NAMES what was driven, so **if a future edit makes the bash consume a second answer, that list goes stale LOUDLY instead of quietly.** A harness that merely spawns the shim without enumerating what it answered would let a second question appear inside a green run, which is the exact failure this bound exists to make impossible — so it is written as an assertion over the enumerated list, never as a comment beside the fixture.

    **(ii) `verify`'s loop runs TWICE** — shim on `PATH`, so every check reaches its subject; and shim ABSENT, so the `opencode-not-on-path` result is pinned identically through both implementations. This is what answers the objection that sank the alternative reading: `verify`'s green figure would otherwise rest on precisely the number this ruling disclaims for `install`.

    **(iii) `repair`, `purge` and `restore` take Decision 30's ORIGINAL condition 2 in full** — the driven loop through both implementations over the same fixture, with tree and report parity — because no gate exists on them to waive. Measured: zero hits for `command -v opencode`, `check_baseline` or `SUPPORTED_OPENCODE_VERSION` across all three scripts.

    **(iv) C3-S4 arms on this**, and nothing in its repair scope was ever waiting on the question.

  - **C3-S4 record — the config / merge / verify-report half, measured 2026-08-31 (DELEGATED — operator's delegate, c3-s4-apply-1).** Generated from the drivers this slice runs, never transcribed.

    **Working verbs and throwing ones.** `oso repair --host opencode` lands COMPLETE: `--yes`, `--list`, the one positional backup name, all six refusals and the restore itself. `install`, `verify` and `purge --host opencode` throw, and `VerbNotImplementedError` now names the slice that brings them — `install --host opencode is not yet implemented in this slice — C3-S4b brings it`. **`verify --host opencode` is in the throwing list rather than the working one, and the reason is structural rather than a choice**: `run_local_checks` emits 17 rows, of which this half owns 5 (one host row, four config rows) and C3-S4b owns 12 — and the 12 include `isolated fixture install`, which every artifact row reads, so a port verifier assembled here would have no subject to read. The 5 rows land as exported functions with their bash counterparts driven against them; C3-S4b assembles them.

    **The ownership rows: seven proved from the bash BEFORE the port reproduced them** (Decision 24c), by sourcing `install-opencode.sh` under a fixture HOME with a fixture `operator-preserved-keys` sink and calling `render_config` directly — one drive per corpus, six drives in all. Every win32 cell in the table above is now a measurement rather than a plan, and the three refusals were driven to their exits with their messages compared word for word.

    **Four divergences the port carries, each measured and none of them silent.** (1) **The serialiser.** Python's `json.dump` defaults to `ensure_ascii=True` and writes an operator's `cañón` as `ca\u00f1\u00f3n`; `JSON.stringify` emits UTF-8. Both decode to the same document, and an ASCII corpus renders byte-identically through both implementations — pinned as a case rather than left for a later reader to rediscover. (2) **A restore this port refuses where the bash crashes mid-write.** `restore_missing_keys` walks `target.setdefault(name, {})`; when the live config holds a non-object where a container should be, the bash raises a `TypeError` after `set -e` has already left the operator's file half-considered. `restoreBlockedBy` names the key and refuses before writing anything. (3) **The command report's GRAMMAR.** C3-D2 pins the VERIFY report shape; the command verbs use `renderCommandReport`, so repair's report is the port's grammar rather than the bash's `[oso-code]` lines. What was compared instead is the FACTS — the restored key paths in order, their count, the snapshot name, the exit code, and the resulting `opencode.json` BYTES. (4) **Usage exits.** The config-home guard keeps the bash's exit 2 (which `verify-opencode.sh`'s own guard row asserts); argv usage errors keep the port's pre-existing exit 1, which is not this slice's to change.

    **The shim's enumerated call list, and a correction to the figure it was armed with.** The harness lists the shim-answered calls and asserts over the list rather than beside it: each entry names the bash function that spawns the call, the file that defines it, and the caller that reaches it, and the case reads all three by CONTENT. Measured by a shim that logs every argv it is asked: **one question, two call sites**, not one. `probe_opencode_version` in `install-opencode.sh`, reached from `check_baseline`, is the site Decision 34 names, and it is the only one an install drive answers — confirmed, exactly one logged line. The second is `opencode_version_of` in `bootstrap/lib/opencode-verification.sh`, reached from `verify-opencode.sh`'s `opencode_version_status`. **Decision 34's "exactly one" is right about the INSTALL and short by one about a corpus that also drives verify**, which is this slice's corpus.

    **The parity harness, its corpus and the red it was shown.** One fixture HOME staged by `bash install-opencode.sh --yes --no-impeccable --no-git-hook` over a seeded operator config and AGENTS.md, copied once per leg, on a PATH built by filtering every directory holding an `opencode` executable out of the ambient one and prepending the shim — so no leg can reach an installed host binary (G4), asserted rather than assumed. `repair` takes Decision 30's condition 2 in full: both implementations over the same fixture, byte-identical `opencode.json`, identical restored-key list and order, identical count, identical snapshot name, identical exit, plus six refusals compared by message. `verify`'s loop runs TWICE — shim on PATH, where the version row reads the pin; shim absent, where `opencode-not-on-path` is what both implementations return. **The shim-absent run also corrects a reading of nightly's figure**: driving the whole bash verifier without a shim reports `passed: 16, failed: 0` against 17 with one, and the ONE row that moves is the version row, because `setup_local_fixture` manufactures its own shim inside its own fixture. Each agreement was shown failing first: a port document with a preserved key dropped, a port that returned one key fewer, a table with a row removed, and three deliberately damaged fixtures whose rows both implementations then call `malformed`, `dropped` and `divergent`.

    **The row enumeration is read from the bash, never asserted.** `OPENCODE_LOCAL_CHECK_ROWS` is compared against the check names extracted from `run_local_checks`, `run_ts_bar` and `run_shell_syntax` in call order; a row the bash grows and the table does not carry fails the case. The 12 rows this half defers are named individually rather than counted.

    **The flag table is HOST × VERB and nothing is dropped.** `FLAGS_PER_HOST_AND_VERB` replaces `FLAGS_PER_HOST`, populated from each bash `case` block: `verify` takes no arguments on any host (as every bash verifier does), `repair --host opencode` takes `--yes --list` and one positional, `purge --host opencode` takes all four of its flags with `--restore` carrying its directory. The seven ordered exclusion pairs, the repeat guard and the arity guard are DATA with one refusal case each, and **`--keep-gentle-ai --restore DIR` is accepted while `--restore DIR --keep-gentle-ai` is refused**, which is the measured asymmetry kept rather than smoothed. `purge --host codex` is left at `--yes` alone: adding `--restore` there would offer a flag `purgeCodex` ignores, and Decision 34 routed that item to C3-S5.

    **Per-kind ratios, labelled with their denominators.** Repair/config: **616** port lines (`opencode.ts` 319, `opencode-config.ts` 166, `cli.ts` +126, `report.ts` +4, `pins.ts` +1) ÷ **1,075** whole-file bash lines (`repair-opencode.sh` 215 + `install-opencode.sh` 860) = **0.57**. Verify: **199** port lines (`verify-opencode.ts` 155, `opencode-host.ts` 44) ÷ **654** gross = **0.30**, ÷ **465** net of the 189-line C4-routed wave-smoke block = **0.43**. **Both are LOWER bounds on this half's own ratio and are labelled so rather than read as final**: each denominator carries a whole bash file whose other half C3-S4b ports, and C3-S4b's numerator lands against the same denominator.
